## [HoloCount: New Benchmark for Visual Counting in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06420) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HoloCount is a new benchmark for evaluating visual counting in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), structured around a three-level taxonomy: semantic counting, analytical counting, and robustness testing. The authors evaluated over 20 state-of-the-art MLLMs and found a significant performance gap as tasks move from perception to complex reasoning and adverse scenarios. This benchmark addresses a known limitation of MLLMs—numerical hallucinations—by providing a structured taxonomy that captures complex failure modes. It offers a systematic landscape of current counting capabilities and a roadmap for developing more grounded and reliable multimodal systems, which is crucial for applications requiring precise quantitative understanding. HoloCount includes three task categories: semantic counting (atomic and property-based enumeration), analytical counting (spatial and set-based reasoning), and robustness testing (high-density scenes and linguistic biases). The dataset is publicly available at https://mm-mvr.github.io/HoloCount/.

**Background**: Visual counting in MLLMs requires integrating fine-grained grounding and spatial reasoning, but current models often suffer from numerical hallucinations. Existing benchmarks focus on basic perception in simplified contexts, failing to capture failures under logical constraints or adversarial conditions. HoloCount aims to fill this gap by providing a holistic and diagnostically rich evaluation framework.

## [Grounding LLMs in Wastewater Simulators for Causal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05151) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper compares three methods for grounding a frozen Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct model in a wastewater simulator (CCSS-IX), achieving 99.5% accuracy with a live simulator oracle, 79% with structured parameter injection, and 75.8% with a Decoupled Recall-Reasoning (DRR) retriever on a 198-question causal benchmark. The DRR retriever, with 110M parameters, trains in ~17 seconds per plant and transfers to a biologically distinct plant with 88% accuracy. This work provides a practical deployment ladder for grounding LLMs in industrial causal reasoning, showing that even a lightweight retriever can outperform strong RAG baselines. It has significant implications for decision support in wastewater treatment and potentially other industrial domains where causal understanding is critical. On a 60-question counterfactual benchmark, only Method 3 (DRR) handles intervention queries, outperforming Method 2 by +16.3 percentage points (95% CI [+7.1, +26.4]). The DRR retriever also shows out-of-domain generalization on ARC, reaching 79% versus 76% for unconstrained Llama-3.1-8B and 74% for full-injection.

A new arXiv paper compares RoBERTa-based sentiment analysis with an LLM-based multi-dimensional framing analysis on 50 political news articles from 17 outlets, revealing that RoBERTa classifies 70% of articles as neutral—a phenomenon the authors call 'neutral collapse.' The LLM approach captures bias, sensationalism, and framing, offering richer insights. This finding challenges the adequacy of traditional sentiment analysis for political discourse research, which is central to social sciences and humanities. It suggests that LLM-based frameworks can provide more epistemologically aligned tools for analyzing political media, potentially reshaping how researchers approach computational text analysis. The study found that 23% of neutral-classified articles had negative probability scores above 0.30, indicating hidden negativity. The LLM-based platform outputs multi-dimensional metrics including political bias direction and intensity, sensationalism, emotional appeal, and political framing, which align with SSH epistemologies.

## [Study Finds Global Region and Status Bias in AI Academic Gatekeeping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05178) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a controlled simulation framework where LLM-based professors must grant access to only one requester, revealing that models exhibit contrasting academic status biases and systematic regional preferences. The study shows that frontier LLMs favor Global South requesters due to pro-equity safety alignment, while open-weight and small models often favor the Global North. This research highlights how normative assumptions embedded in LLMs can shape gatekeeping decisions in academia, affecting equitable access to scientific resources. It underscores the importance of auditing AI systems for fairness and value alignment, with implications for deploying LLMs in academic and professional settings. The study varied requesters by global region (Global North vs. Global South) and academic seniority (undergraduate, PhD candidate, postdoc, tenured professor) while keeping all other factors constant. Results showed that some LLMs prioritize PhD candidates, others favor tenured professors, and regional bias diverges based on model architecture, with open-weight models reflecting unaligned geographic distribution in pre-training data.

**Background**: Academic gatekeeping refers to decisions about who gets access to scarce resources like paywalled articles, datasets, or CVs, often made informally by professors. The Global North broadly comprises Northern America, Europe, Israel, Japan, South Korea, Australia, and New Zealand, while the Global South includes regions like Africa, Latin America, and parts of Asia. LLMs are trained on vast internet data, which may contain geographic and status biases that influence their outputs.

## [In-Context Forcing Improves Few-Step Autoregressive Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05237) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces In-Context Forcing, a progressive autoregressive paradigm that uses contexts with decreasing noise levels to improve temporal consistency and dynamics in few-step video diffusion models. This approach decouples the strict dependence on previous clean frames, enabling cross-frame parallel denoising and faster inference. This method addresses a known limitation in few-step autoregressive video diffusion models, where clean context frames cause shortcuts and compromise temporal semantics. By improving both visual fidelity and inference speed, it could advance real-time video generation and streaming applications. The method applies less masking to distant frames and more masking to adjacent ones, providing adaptive guidance. Experiments on VBench show significant improvements over state-of-the-art approaches in both visual fidelity and inference speed.

In-Context Forcing draws inspiration from diffusion as masking, exploring the impact of noisy contexts to balance consistency and dynamics.

## [One Qubit Can Beat One Bit: Quantum Advantage for Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05240) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces Quantum Random Access Quantization (QRAQ), a framework that encodes context-dependent signs in a quantum random-access code and retrieves them via context-matched Pauli measurements, overcoming the shared-sign constraint in one-bit post-training quantization. The authors prove a row-wise separation from shared-sign one-bit PTQ, showing that QRAQ achieves strictly lower ideal reconstruction risk when optimal context-wise signs are incompatible. This work provides a theoretical quantum advantage in a practical machine learning task, potentially inspiring new quantum-inspired algorithms for model compression. It also clarifies that the resource enabling this advantage is measurement incompatibility rather than quantization alone, which could reshape how quantum advantages are understood in ML contexts. The paper derives finite-shot and calibrated-noise conditions under which the separation is retained, and notes that fixed-readout quantum schemes are classically simulable. It also characterizes the role of scale granularity and provides finite-sample certificates, with simulator experiments validating the predicted ideal, finite-shot, noisy, and multi-context regimes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces model size by converting weights to low-bit representations, with one-bit PTQ using only the sign of each weight. However, this forces all deployment contexts to share the same binary weight matrix, even when activation statistics differ. Quantum random access codes (QRACs) encode classical bits into a smaller quantum state, allowing any single bit to be retrieved with some probability, and are known to outperform classical random access codes. This paper leverages QRACs to encode context-dependent signs, offering a theoretical advantage over classical shared-sign approaches.

## [PRISM: A Data Synthesis Framework for Priority-Aware Multimodal Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05249) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PRISM introduces a four-stage data synthesis framework that trains multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to execute prioritized rubrics, along with PRISM-Eval, a judge-free evaluation metric. With only 10K synthesized samples, PRISM improves Qwen3-VL-4B's Strict accuracy from 9.5% to 30.1% on PRISM-Eval while preserving general benchmark performance. This work addresses a critical gap in multimodal instruction following, where real-world instructions often bundle multiple requirements with unequal importance, but existing training data typically treats them as single self-contained questions. By enabling models to follow prioritized rubrics, PRISM offers a scalable path toward more robust and practical multimodal assistants, benefiting both researchers and downstream applications. PRISM's four stages include persona-task pair generation, prefix-guided rule set creation, quality-filtered rubric synthesis, and structured verification trace generation. PRISM-Eval uses deterministic matching against fixed labels for Loose and Strict metrics, eliminating the need for an inference-time judge model. The gains transfer to four additional open-source MLLMs across dense and MoE architectures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal instruction following typically involves training models on datasets where each instruction is a single question with a single answer. However, real-world instructions often contain multiple requirements with different priorities, requiring models to verify each rule before making a judgment. PRISM introduces the concept of 'rubric comprehension,' where the model acts as an executor that follows a prioritized rubric rather than a generator measured against one. This approach is part of a broader trend in multimodal LLM research to improve instruction following with more structured and realistic training data.

## [Personalized Tax-Aware Portfolio App Using RL, MoE, and LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05255) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers have built a fully integrated retail portfolio management application that uses a three-phase reinforcement learning system—a self-supervised cross-asset encoder, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) allocation policy with an intent router, and a LoRA adapter—to generate personalized, tax-aware recommendations from natural language goals. The system is integration-tested against Alpaca's paper-trading API and includes features like multi-user authentication and a preview-before-apply flow. This work addresses a significant gap for retail investors, who typically lack access to personalized, tax-aware portfolio management that institutional clients enjoy. By combining advanced ML techniques with a practical, broker-integrated application, it could democratize sophisticated investment strategies and influence future fintech developments. The system uses a FastAPI backend and web dashboard, routes goals to one of six investment mandates, and includes a trust-first preview-before-apply confirmation flow, daily email digests, and an auditable action-integrity chain. Validation is preliminary, based on 14-day walk-forward backtests with bootstrapped confidence intervals, and the authors report practical engineering lessons such as silently-inactive integration paths and hanging third-party API calls.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) is a machine learning paradigm where agents learn to make decisions by interacting with an environment to maximize cumulative rewards. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is an architecture that divides a model into specialized sub-networks (experts) and routes inputs to the most relevant ones, improving efficiency and performance. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a technique for efficiently fine-tuning large models by adding small trainable matrices, enabling personalization without retraining the entire model. Robo-advisors are automated platforms that provide financial advice, but many rely on static, rule-based allocations, lacking the personalization and tax awareness of institutional systems.

The safety formulation and FailBench provide a foundation for developing safer motion plans and learned policies, potentially accelerating real-world adoption. FailBench includes diverse failure modes such as sensing issues and actuator malfunctions, and is based on MuJoCo.

## [Multi-Agent Transformer for Queue-Level XR Traffic Scheduling in TSN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05340) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes a multi-agent reinforcement learning approach using a multi-agent transformer (MAT) to schedule queue-level XR traffic in TSN networks, addressing inter-queue dependencies and non-periodic traffic. Simulation results show up to 71.42% latency reduction and 83.2% failure rate reduction compared to baselines. This work addresses critical limitations in existing TSN scheduling methods, which often assume periodic traffic and use centralized or coarse-grained control. By enabling implicit coordination across heterogeneous co-located XR applications, it could improve real-time performance in MEC environments, benefiting applications like augmented and virtual reality. The method models each TSN queue as an agent and uses attention over agents' observations and actions to capture inter-queue dependencies. The paper is a preprint (arXiv:2608.05340) and has not yet been peer-reviewed or validated by the community.

Mobile Edge Computing (MEC) brings computation closer to users, supporting latency-sensitive applications like extended reality (XR). However, co-located XR services in MEC can contend for network resources, and XR traffic is often non-periodic with varying criticality, making scheduling challenging. Traditional TSN scheduling often relies on static or centralized methods that assume periodic traffic, which may not adapt well to dynamic XR workloads.

## [APQF: Agentic Profiling-Guided Pruning and Quantization Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05499) ⭐️ 7.0/10

APQF is a novel automated framework that combines agentic profiling with LLM planners to perform structured pruning and mixed-precision quantization, along with adaptive fine-tuning, in a single pipeline. It is claimed to be the first to integrate LLM-guided, profiling-grounded decisions with a fully training-aware pipeline for both CNNs and vision transformers. This framework addresses the practical challenge of model compression on edge devices by automating expert decisions, potentially reducing manual effort and improving accuracy retention. It demonstrates significant compute reductions (13-18x) on ImageNet while maintaining accuracy, which could accelerate deployment of efficient deep learning models. The framework uses a profiling agent to measure cost distribution and sensitivity, guiding LLM planners to propose per-layer pruning ratios, bit-widths, and recovery strategies, which are validated before execution. Evaluations on ResNet, VGG7, ViT, DeiT, and Swin show that on ImageNet it reduces bit-operations to 5.6-7.7% of baseline, and on VGG7 it achieves 93.15% accuracy with only 0.41% of baseline bit-operations, improving on the full-precision baseline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Structured pruning removes entire structures like channels or layers, unlike unstructured pruning which removes individual weights and requires special hardware support. Mixed-precision quantization assigns different bit-widths to different layers to balance accuracy and efficiency. Agentic profiling refers to AI-driven performance analysis that guides optimization decisions, often using LLMs to automate the process.

## [Vibe Compiler: A Tool to Preserve Human Agency in AI-Assisted Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05545) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Vibe Compiler, a research-logic synthesis tool based on the Synthesis-Analysis Reciprocity Model, which helps researchers transform vague ideas into coherent research logic without requiring prompt engineering. The tool uses a research paper ontology of sixteen academic parameters and prompts reflective questions when compilation fails, rather than autonomously filling gaps. This tool addresses the critical issue of preserving human epistemic agency in AI-assisted intellectual work, which is increasingly important as generative AI becomes more prevalent. It could influence how researchers and educators design AI tools to enhance metacognition rather than encourage passive acceptance of AI-generated reasoning. The Vibe Compiler is built on NotebookLM and Gemini, and the authors emphasize that effective AI-assisted reasoning depends more on the knowledge structure provided to the AI than on sophisticated prompting. The framework characterizes structural gaps along two dimensions: cognitive function (Synthesis vs. Analysis) and executing agent (human vs. AI), yielding four origin types.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Generative AI tools like large language models can accelerate intellectual work but risk eroding human agency by encouraging uncritical acceptance of AI-generated reasoning. The Synthesis-Analysis Reciprocity Model views intellectual construction as a reciprocal interaction between synthesis (combining components) and analysis (critically evaluating them), which underpins the Vibe Compiler's design. The tool aims to keep researchers as managers who critically direct and validate AI-generated reasoning.

## [Turing's 1948 Chess Game as Human-Approximates-Machine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05558) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper reinterprets Turing's 1948 report 'Intelligent Machinery' as the conceptual source for later imitation games, arguing that the chess-based game can be seen as a human-approximates-machine game. It identifies design concepts such as machine mistakes, exclusion of physical features, the human judge's role, and search as core to intellectual activity. This analysis offers new historical and conceptual insights into the origins of the imitation game and AI, suggesting the framework can be used to examine when human intelligence becomes machine-like under task constraints. It enriches the philosophical understanding of Turing's ideas and their relevance to modern AI discussions. The paper's second contribution argues that restricting the human contestant to a poor chess player increases the role of intellectual search and makes human behavior more comparable to machine behavior. This interpretation presents the 1948 game as a human-approximates-machine game, expanding the imitation game framework beyond the traditional machine-imitates-human question.

A new preprint study (arXiv:2608.05583) reveals that LLMs agree with humans that patients bear responsibility for health-harming behaviors, but they refuse to let this judgment influence resource allocation, defaulting to random allocation instead of favoring less-culpable patients as humans do. This finding highlights a systematic difference between LLM and human moral reasoning in high-stakes healthcare decisions, which could impact the deployment of LLMs in clinical settings. It also suggests that increasing model capability may amplify normative disagreement with humans, raising concerns about alignment. The study evaluated a wide range of LLMs across different model families and capability levels using clinical vignettes adapted from prior studies. LLMs also placed greater emphasis on access to information, reducing responsibility judgments when health-risk knowledge was unavailable.

## [SJRL: Search-Aided RL for Robust Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05588) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces SJRL, a novel method that combines reinforcement learning with search-based planning to address the LMAPF-R2 problem, which includes robust safety and in-place rotation constraints. SJRL jointly optimizes agent and environment policies, achieving significant improvements over the strong Causal-PIBT baseline on high-density maps. This work addresses a realistic and challenging variant of multi-agent path finding that incorporates motion constraints critical for real-world warehouse automation. By improving coordination in constrained spaces, SJRL has the potential to enhance the efficiency and safety of automated warehouse systems, impacting robotics and AI planning communities. SJRL augments neural policies with Causal PIBT, a single-step search-based planner, and introduces a unified RL formulation that jointly optimizes agent and environment policies. The environment policy learns graph edge costs to provide global movement guidance via backward Dijkstra search. Experiments validate SJRL in a mixed-reality warehouse with 8 physical robots and 248 virtual robots.

Many learning-based planners assume simplified kinematics, but real-world systems often require robust safety and in-place rotation constraints. SJRL combines reinforcement learning with search-based planning to handle these constraints effectively.

## [GAUGE: Fine-Grained Counterfactual Gating for Incomplete Multimodal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05608) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GAUGE introduces a lightweight counterfactual gating framework that uses prediction-aware Taylor evidence scores to selectively retain reliable evidence units in incomplete multimodal classification. It computes counterfactual effects in a single forward-backward pass and converts them into additive attention-logit biases for unit-wise evidence modulation. This work addresses a critical real-world problem where multimodal inputs are often incomplete, and existing methods operate at a coarse modality level, failing to retain reliable components while suppressing misleading ones. By enabling fine-grained evidence control, GAUGE could improve prediction reliability across various applications such as healthcare, robotics, and multimedia analysis, and sets a precedent for principled counterfactual reasoning in multimodal learning. GAUGE uses a frozen imputer to fill missing modalities and encodes observed and recovered inputs as fine-grained evidence units. It employs prediction-aware Taylor evidence scores to approximate counterfactual effects without explicit intervention, and a Taylor remainder theoretical analysis characterizes the error of the first-order approximation. Experiments across six benchmarks show that GAUGE outperforms strong baselines in diverse incomplete-input settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal classification typically assumes all modalities are available, but real-world data often suffer from missing modalities. Existing methods use imputation or dynamic fusion to handle incompleteness, but they operate at a coarse modality level, which can retain misleading components within a recovered modality. Counterfactual reasoning offers a principled way to evaluate the effect of replacing evidence units, and Taylor approximations provide a computationally efficient means to estimate such effects.

The experiments cover six stocks and cryptocurrency assets, comparing against 16 competitive baselines across multiple trading metrics, with the reported returns on GOOG and TSLA being particularly high.

The paper introduces SafeDivertor, a task-driven framework that reconstructs divertor heat-flux profiles directly from macroscopic plasma-state signals during discharge, and presents DivMPS2HF, a new benchmark dataset for this task. SafeDivertor achieves the best overall performance among evaluated time-series baselines across all five metrics. This work shifts the paradigm from conventional infrared-based inversion, which is post-discharge and requires complex modeling, to an online signal-based approach, enabling real-time monitoring and control of plasma-facing components. It could significantly improve fusion device safety and operational efficiency, and the new dataset provides a standardized benchmark for future research. SafeDivertor employs physical prior-aware initialization, input perturbation, spectral-aware reconstruction optimization, and progressive training to address key challenges. The source code will be released on GitHub, and the dataset DivMPS2HF is constructed from multi-source discharge data.

## [DistMedVL: Probabilistic Vision-Language Framework for Uncertainty-Aware Medical Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05683) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DistMedVL introduces a probabilistic vision-language framework with a lightweight Probabilistic Cross-Modal Adapter (PCM-Adapter) that explicitly models representational uncertainty in medical image segmentation. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods across eight benchmarks with only 6.3M trainable parameters. This work addresses a critical limitation in existing vision-language segmentation methods that rely on deterministic matching, which fails under domain shift. By modeling aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty, DistMedVL enhances robustness and data efficiency, potentially improving clinical reliability of multimodal medical image analysis. The PCM-Adapter consists of two modules: a Mahalanobis Alignment Module (MAM) that models textual tokens as Gaussian distributions and computes patch-text compatibility via Mahalanobis distance, and a Distribution Flow Module (DFM) that estimates modality-wise confidence and performs vision-guided refinement. The framework uses frozen encoders and achieves superior perturbation robustness and cross-dataset generalization.

**Background**: Medical image segmentation often suffers from uncertainty due to ambiguous boundaries (aleatoric) and limited training data (epistemic). Vision-language models align visual and textual representations, but deterministic alignment ignores these uncertainties, leading to fragile performance under domain shift. DistMedVL introduces probabilistic alignment to explicitly model uncertainty, improving robustness in real-world clinical conditions.

## [Spectral Aliasing Pretext: Self-Supervised Fault Diagnosis via Spectrum Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05705) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Spectral Aliasing Pretext (SAP), a self-supervised learning method that pretrains a Transformer on unlabeled vibration data by deliberately undersampling signals to create a folded spectrum and training the model to reconstruct the original unfolded spectrum. Experiments on the CWRU dataset show that SAP, combined with linear probing, achieves high classification performance with only a small fraction of labeled data and low variance. This work addresses the practical challenge of limited labeled data in industrial fault diagnosis, where deep learning typically requires extensive annotations. By leveraging unlabeled vibration data through a novel pretext task, SAP offers a more data-efficient and reliable alternative to fully supervised training, potentially reducing the cost and effort of deploying deep learning in real-world machinery monitoring. SAP exploits spectral aliasing, a phenomenon where undersampling causes high-frequency components to fold into lower frequencies, creating a distorted spectrum. The pretext task forces the model to learn frequency-domain invariants characteristic of mechanical faults without using potentially destructive data augmentations. The paper reports that full fine-tuning, including fully supervised training, does not yield more stable or better results than SAP with linear probing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) is a machine learning paradigm where models learn representations from unlabeled data by solving a pretext task, such as predicting missing parts or reconstructing inputs. In fault diagnosis, vibration signals are commonly analyzed in the frequency domain, and spectral aliasing is a well-known issue in signal processing that occurs when sampling rate is insufficient. The CWRU dataset is a benchmark for bearing fault diagnosis, widely used to evaluate diagnostic methods.

## [Adaptive LDP Framework ABC Estimates Data Domain Without Prior Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05737) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces the Adaptive Bounding of Clipping regions (ABC) method, an adaptive local differential privacy (LDP) framework that dynamically estimates the data domain by collecting privatized clipping signals from users, avoiding the need for a predefined domain. This addresses a critical practical limitation of LDP mechanisms, which typically require a known data domain; by eliminating this requirement, ABC improves the utility of numerical data collection in real-world scenarios where the domain is unknown, benefiting privacy-preserving data analysis. Each user sends both their perturbed numerical value and a privatized signal indicating whether the original value was clipped by the current domain. The ABC method iteratively adjusts the domain based on aggregated signals, with theoretical proof of convergence and empirical validation across multiple datasets and LDP mechanisms, showing robustness to hyperparameters.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Local Differential Privacy (LDP) is a privacy-preserving technique that allows individuals to perturb their data before sharing it with a collector, ensuring privacy even against the collector. Traditional LDP mechanisms require a predefined data domain, but in practice this domain is often unknown, leading to a trade-off between clipping loss (if the domain is too narrow) and excessive noise (if too wide). The ABC method addresses this by adaptively estimating the domain, which is a significant advancement for practical LDP applications.

## [EchoPrompt: Training-Free LLM Text Detection via Latent Prompt Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05741) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces EchoPrompt, a training-free detector that identifies LLM-generated text by restoring latent prompts and measuring likelihood gains, achieving state-of-the-art performance among zero-shot detectors. It explicitly models the generation mechanism by prepending a unified generic prefix and calibrating against the base model. This work addresses the growing need for robust detection of machine-generated text, which is critical for mitigating misinformation, educational misuse, and platform governance issues. By improving robustness over existing statistical methods, EchoPrompt could enhance the reliability of AI content detection in real-world applications. EchoPrompt restores a generic assistant-response context, measures the induced likelihood gain with an instruction-tuned model, and calibrates it against the corresponding base model. The method is training-free and demonstrates strong robustness across challenging evaluation settings, though it is an incremental advancement rather than a groundbreaking shift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent and convincing text at scale, creating risks for misinformation and misuse. Existing zero-shot detectors often rely on probability-based statistical discrepancies but do not explicitly account for the training process of LLMs, limiting their robustness. EchoPrompt leverages the idea that machine-generated text is conditioned on an upstream prompt, which can be partially reactivated by prepending a unified generic prefix.

## [UniVVT: Unified End-to-End Video Virtual Try-On Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05745) ⭐️ 7.0/10

UniVVT introduces a unified end-to-end framework for video virtual try-on, replacing multi-stage pipelines with a semantic-conditioned generation approach. It leverages a Multimodal Large Language Model to implicitly capture garment transfer semantics, eliminating the need for explicit mask, pose, and warping modules at inference. This work addresses critical limitations of existing video virtual try-on methods, which rely on fragile geometric priors that can propagate errors. By simplifying the pipeline and achieving state-of-the-art performance, UniVVT could accelerate deployment in e-commerce and virtual try-on applications, making the technology more practical and accessible. The framework uses a scene-task perceiver built on a Multimodal Large Language Model to encode source video, target garment, and task instruction into task-aware latent tokens. A lightweight semantic bridge aligns these tokens with a diffusion-based video generator, and a three-stage progressive training strategy (semantic alignment, joint task adaptation, flexible-resolution refinement) ensures robust coupling.

**Background**: Video Virtual Try-On (VVT) synthesizes a video of a person wearing a target garment while preserving identity, motion, and scene dynamics. Traditional approaches treat VVT as mask-conditioned video inpainting, requiring separate modules for human parsing, pose estimation, and garment warping, which complicates deployment and risks error propagation. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) extend LLMs to process and generate content across multiple modalities, such as text, images, and video, enabling richer semantic understanding.

The paper introduces HyTBE, a hyperbolic target-background expert model that mitigates target-background relation shift for cross-domain infrared small target detection (IRSTD). It combines a relation intervention strategy, hyperbolic relation modeling in a Poincaré ball, and a hyperbolic-guided mixture-of-experts adapter to improve generalization across unseen infrared domains. Cross-domain generalization is a critical challenge in IRSTD, as detectors often fail when applied to new infrared domains. HyTBE's novel use of hyperbolic geometry to model target-background relations could inspire more robust detection systems for real-world applications where domain shift is common. HyTBE performs leave-one-domain-out experiments on NUAA-SIRST, NUDT-SIRST, and IRSTD-1K, achieving stronger cross-domain generalization than competitive baselines. The model uses a Target-Background Relation Intervention to perturb targets or backgrounds, and a Hyperbolic-guided MoE Adapter to calibrate multi-scale features.

## [TIPEX: Unifying Replica and Structural Parallelism for Multi-Agent LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05791) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces TIPEX, a controllable execution framework that unifies replica parallelism and structural parallelism in multi-agent LLM systems, enabling systematic combination and analysis of different parallel strategies. Experiments on the GAIA benchmark show that inference-time parallelism can significantly improve accuracy and reduce latency at the cost of increased token consumption. This work addresses a critical gap in understanding how different forms of parallelism interact in multi-agent LLM systems, which are increasingly used for complex reasoning tasks. By providing a unified framework, TIPEX could help practitioners optimize inference efficiency and cost, potentially impacting the deployment of LLM-based agents in real-world applications. TIPEX distinguishes between replica parallelism (exploring multiple complete solution paths at task level) and structural parallelism (concurrent execution within a single solution path via task decomposition). The framework supports systematic combinations and analyses of parallel strategies and parameter configurations, and experiments reveal that tasks of intermediate difficulty benefit most from coordination, while overly aggressive parallelism does not always yield better performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent LLM systems involve multiple model invocations and complex coordination during inference, and their execution strategies directly affect accuracy, latency, and computational cost. Parallel execution is a common technique to improve inference efficiency, but existing approaches often treat different forms of parallelism in isolation. This paper provides a unified perspective by modeling parallelism as two levels of decision processes, offering a systematic way to study their interplay.

## [FERL: Evidential Fuzzy Rule Learning with Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05859) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Fast Evidential Rule Learning (FERL), a method that learns interpretable fuzzy rule classifiers with native evidential outputs and abstention capabilities. FERL achieves statistically significant accuracy improvements over state-of-the-art rule learners on a 30-dataset tabular benchmark, with +2.6% average accuracy over the second best. FERL addresses the need for interpretable models that can express uncertainty and abstain when uncertain, which is crucial for high-stakes applications like healthcare and finance. Its native evidential outputs and strong empirical performance could make it a practical choice for deploying transparent AI systems. FERL's belief, plausibility, and abstention capabilities arise directly from fuzzy memberships in a single deterministic pass, without auxiliary heads or held-out sets. The method is theoretically Lipschitz stable, ensuring smooth evidential outputs, and it also performs well on out-of-distribution detection and concept-bottleneck tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Interpretable classification models are often preferred in domains where decisions must be explainable, but they typically lack mechanisms to express uncertainty or abstain. Fuzzy rule classifiers use if-then rules with fuzzy logic to make decisions, and evidential learning extends this by providing belief and plausibility measures. Abstention allows models to refrain from making predictions when confidence is low, improving reliability.

## [D-CLOT: Double Closed-Loop Optimal Transport Boosts Unsupervised Action Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05877) ⭐️ 7.0/10

D-CLOT introduces a double closed-loop optimal transport method that re-estimates action prototypes from refined frame embeddings, addressing the representation–prototype inconsistency in existing OT-based approaches. It achieves up to +12.7 F1 and +10.2 mIoU per-video gains on the YTI benchmark over CLOT. This work improves unsupervised action segmentation, a key task in video understanding, by making prototype updates consistent with the refined representation geometry. It also establishes the first unsupervised baseline on the fine-grained Assembly101 benchmark, potentially advancing research in procedural activity recognition. D-CLOT uses a graph-constrained module to regularize OT-refined frame and segment representations, preserving local neighborhood geometry. Two variants are proposed: D-CLOT updates prototypes via k-means, while D-CLOT_B uses OT barycenters weighted by the refined transport plan, showing complementary and robust improvements across five benchmarks.

However, existing methods often fail to re-estimate prototypes from refined frame geometry, leading to inconsistencies, especially for ambiguous transitions and short actions. CLOT introduced a closed-loop mechanism to refine frame embeddings, and D-CLOT extends this by adding a second loop for prototype re-estimation.

## [Em-dash frequency doubles in 2025 congressional press releases, hinting at LLM use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05889) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A preregistered analysis of 146,239 U.S. congressional press releases from 2021-2025 found that the density of unspaced em-dashes per 1,000 characters doubled in 2025 to 0.217, up from a stable 0.10-0.12 baseline in 2021-2024. The share of releases containing such an em-dash rose from ~13% to 24.8%. This study provides a measurable, population-level stylistic marker that could indicate broad adoption of LLM-assisted writing in political communication. It contributes to the growing field of LLM detection and raises questions about transparency and authenticity in public discourse. The primary frequency ratio (2023-2025 vs 2021-2022) was 1.55 (95% CI 1.28-1.93), just above the prespecified 1.5x threshold, but the full preregistered decision rule was not met, so the interpretation is exploratory. The rise was net-new (hyphen density stable), held within authors and a closed panel, and survived falsification tests, but no step at the ChatGPT cut-off was found; instead, a post-period acceleration was observed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) can leave subtle stylistic traces in text, and the unspaced em-dash (word---word) is a known example because it is common in typeset prose but unusual in U.S. press writing, where AP style calls for spaced dashes. Preregistration is a practice in science where hypotheses and analysis plans are registered before conducting the study to prevent p-hacking and increase transparency. The study used Poisson/negative-binomial models with a length offset to account for varying text lengths, a standard approach for analyzing frequency data.

This paper introduces BALANCE, a hybrid framework that combines autoregressive decoding (AD) and speculative decoding (SD) for edge LLM inference, jointly optimizing user scheduling and resource allocation to maximize task throughput under latency and memory constraints. BALANCE addresses the latency-memory tradeoff in edge LLM inference, enabling more efficient service for heterogeneous user demands under limited resources. This could significantly improve the scalability and performance of edge AI systems in next-generation mobile networks. The problem is NP-hard, so the authors propose a polynomial-time algorithm that decomposes it into two sub-problems and achieves a constant approximation guarantee. Experiments show BALANCE consistently outperforms conventional AD and SD in task throughput.

SkillMemo introduces a novel framework that decomposes long-horizon demonstrations into latent atomic skills using a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture and stores them in a dynamic episodic memory bank, enhancing compositional generalization in embodied manipulation. The framework consistently improves both Diffusion Policy and Vision-Language-Action backbones, achieving state-of-the-art performance on simulation and real-world tasks. This work addresses a critical limitation in embodied AI: the scarcity of large-scale trajectory data limits compositional generalization in out-of-distribution scenarios. By enabling reusable skill structures and memory retrieval, SkillMemo could significantly improve the adaptability and efficiency of robot learning systems, impacting both research and practical deployment. The framework includes an expert-guided trajectory segmentation module based on MoE, which implicitly partitions trajectories into skill primitives represented by gating coefficients. A skill-level episodic memory stores these as key-value pairs, and during inference, the most relevant skills are retrieved and fused with the current gating distribution to refine action predictions. Experiments show SkillMemo outperforms π0.5 and exhibits strong generalization to unseen task configurations.

The generated programs are used only for representation pretraining and are not required to faithfully simulate the environment; inaccuracies are corrected through online learning. ## [Agentic Pipeline Grounds VLM Shadow Removal in Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06075) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes an agentic candidate-selection pipeline that grounds commercial vision-language models in shadow-formation physics for shadow removal, achieving a CDD of 0.0075 on the ShadowRemovalRefine benchmark, a 47% reduction over prior methods. This work addresses a timely question of whether commercial vision-language models can replace classic physics-informed low-level vision, showing that physics priors remain crucial for constraining generation. It provides a practical pipeline that improves reliability and could influence future agentic image editing systems. The pipeline involves generating a guided probe, screening for failures, retrying, sampling multiple candidates, filtering, and selecting a final result. Prompting the generator and evaluator to treat shadows as illumination effects rather than material or object structure measurably improves quality and consistency.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Shadow removal is a classic low-level vision problem influenced by scene geometry, illumination, materials, and occluders, where paired data is scarce. Commercial vision-language models have broad visual priors but can hallucinate or produce physically wrong edits. Physics-informed priors help constrain such underconstrained generation.

## [Latent Context in IRL for Arctic Shipping: Controlled Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06105) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper presents a controlled evaluation of inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) methods for Arctic shipping navigation, comparing linear, nonlinear, and latent-context reward models on 3,186 AIS-derived voyages from 202 vessels. The nonlinear reward improves held-out likelihood by 50.9% over the linear baseline, while adding latent context reduces performance by 16.5%. This research addresses a critical question in meta-IRL: whether latent context variables capture genuine hidden preferences or merely re-encode observed state. The findings challenge the value of latent context in safety-critical domains like Arctic shipping, where interpretable and robust reward models are essential for trustworthy AI deployment. The study uses a pre-registered feature-hiding ablation and context probes to show that vessel-level variation is largely explained by observable route and environmental conditions. It also finds that predictive accuracy, route fidelity, and reward transfer yield different model rankings, indicating no single metric suffices for evaluating learned rewards.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) recovers reward functions from observed behavior, which is useful for AI navigation in dynamic environments like Arctic shipping. Meta-IRL methods introduce latent context variables to capture behavioral heterogeneity across agents, but it is unclear if these variables add value beyond observed state features. This study uses AIS data, which provides vessel trajectories, to evaluate these methods in a real-world domain.

## [Self-Pretraining Boosts Medical Time-Series Transformers, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06122) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new study systematically evaluates self-pretraining (SPT) on transformer models for three medical time-series tasks, showing consistent accuracy improvements of 0-6 percentage points across datasets and model sizes. This finding suggests SPT is a simple, general strategy to improve transformer performance in data-limited clinical settings, potentially reducing the need for large labeled datasets and task-specific architectural changes. The study uses four masking-based objectives for SPT and varies model depth, finding that deeper models benefit more from SPT. Gains are observed in both multivariate and univariate inputs, indicating broad applicability.

It has shown promise in domains like speaker verification, but its effectiveness for medical time-series was unclear. This study addresses that gap by testing SPT on rehabilitation robotics, stress detection, and Parkinson's disease detection tasks.

## [Hardware Keystores Secure AI Agent Signing with Zero-Trust MCP Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06130) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2608.06130) proposes a zero-trust MCP enforcement architecture that uses hardware keystores (HSM, TPM, smart card) via a vendor-neutral PKCS#11 interface to secure AI agent signing workflows. The approach reduces baseline attack success rate from 19.3% to 0% across 12 injection scenarios. This addresses a critical security gap where AI agents store private keys in software-accessible locations, vulnerable to exfiltration. By enforcing hardware confinement and content-aware authorization, it could significantly reduce key theft risks in AI-driven workflows, impacting developers and enterprises relying on automated signing. The architecture includes a five-layer zero-trust stack: session identity (SAGA), scope bounds (Smax), semantic validation (RAV), taint tracking, and hardware execution boundary. Evaluation used four LLM models (gpt-oss-120b, Qwen2.5-72B, DeepSeek-V4-Flash) with n=192, achieving 0% ASR with zero false positives.

Hardware keystores such as HSMs, TPMs, and smart cards execute cryptographic operations on-device, exposing only results via opaque handles. PKCS#11 is a standard C API for interfacing with such hardware, widely used in security applications.

This paper introduces the SheetSage-A2S dataset, containing 61 hours of audio with **kern score encodings for 9,468 clips from 6,066 popular songs, and proposes an improved audio-to-score (A2S) model using MuQ pre-trained features and data augmentation. The model achieves a 4.98% symbol error rate (SER) on classical music, significantly outperforming the previous state-of-the-art's 15.3% SER, and sets a benchmark of 20.92% SER on the new popular music dataset. This work addresses the underexplored application of A2S transcription to popular music by providing the first dedicated dataset, which can catalyze research in this area. The significant improvement on classical music also demonstrates the effectiveness of combining pre-trained features and data augmentation, potentially advancing the state of the art in music information retrieval. The model uses MuQ, a self-supervised music encoder that requires 24 kHz audio input and fp32 inference to avoid NaN issues. The dataset and code are publicly available on GitHub, and the model achieves 20.92% SER on popular music, serving as a strong baseline for future work.

## [PRISM: Distribution-Gated Flow Matching for Controllable Unpaired Image Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06240) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PRISM introduces a GAN-free flow-matching framework that replaces global control with a learned per-feature gate for unpaired image-to-image translation. The gate determines what to preserve or change based on the feature's distance to the target distribution, and it can be overridden locally at inference via text or detectors. This work addresses a key limitation of existing diffusion-based unpaired translators that use a single global control, enabling more precise and controllable image translation. It demonstrates strong performance across multiple benchmarks, including biomedical applications, which could impact fields like virtual staining and histopathology. The gate's spatial prior is derived from each source feature's standardized distance to the target feature distribution, and it controls both the initialization and the transport timing during ODE integration. The corruption is task-matched, using content-anchored AdaIN for structure-preserving translation and partially anchored for structure-changing translation. PRISM achieves the best Inception FID and KID on four out of five benchmarks and the closest nuclei-count ratio on histopathology.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Unpaired image-to-image translation aims to map images from one domain to another without paired examples, requiring decisions about what to change and preserve. Traditional diffusion-based methods often apply a global noise or guidance value, which cannot separate content to keep from appearance to change. Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that learns an ODE to transport samples from a source distribution to a target distribution, offering an alternative to GANs and diffusion models. AdaIN (Adaptive Instance Normalization) is a technique used to align the mean and standard deviation of feature maps, often employed in style transfer.

## [Expert-Validated Bangla Sign Dataset and Lightweight Model Achieve 96.37% Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06252) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces RSBdSL38, a new expert-validated dataset of 10,874 Bangla sign language images covering all 38 hand signs, and proposes a lightweight attention-based CNN with 298,470 parameters that achieves 96.37% accuracy when trained from scratch. The model outperforms or matches heavier pretrained backbones while being significantly more efficient, and it can run on a commodity smartphone at 3.98 ms per image after quantization. This work addresses a real-world accessibility problem by enabling automatic Bangla Sign Language recognition on personal devices, which could improve access to education and services for deaf and hard-of-hearing people in Bangladesh. The lightweight model and expert-validated dataset provide a practical foundation for deploying sign language recognition in resource-constrained settings, potentially inspiring similar efforts for other low-resource sign languages. The model uses grouped bottleneck residual blocks, channel and spatial attention, a multi-scale depthwise hand-feature block, dual pooling, and Swish activations. It achieves 96.37% accuracy on RSBdSL38, within 1.08 percentage points of the best pretrained architecture while using 8.5 to 68x fewer parameters and 1.3 to 21.7x fewer MACs; it also generalizes to six public benchmarks, and a signer-independent split yields 85.18% accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Bangla Sign Language (BdSL) is the primary communication method for deaf and hard-of-hearing people in Bangladesh, but existing recognition systems rely on controlled datasets without expert verification and use heavyweight pretrained models unsuitable for on-device deployment. Lightweight neural networks, such as those using depthwise separable convolutions and attention mechanisms, are designed to reduce computational cost while maintaining accuracy, making them suitable for mobile devices. The RSBdSL38 dataset was collected from real signers at three special-needs schools and validated by experts, addressing the gap in realistic, deployable sign language recognition.

This paper introduces a reference-free framework that uses LLM judges to evaluate the consistency, complexity, and policy coverage of conversational agent benchmarks, providing actionable diagnostics. The framework is reference-free, meaning it does not require ground-truth answers, and uses LLM judges to score benchmarks on consistency, complexity, and policy coverage. It was tested across multiple domains and judge models, and also applied to manually curated benchmarks, demonstrating its broad applicability.

## [TYTAN: Automated Analytic Semantic Schema Construction via Neurosymbolic LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06331) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TYTAN is a new system that automatically constructs analytic semantic schemas from relational databases by combining symbolic analysis with LLM-based semantic inference, and it asks targeted natural-language questions when decisions are ambiguous. In evaluations on eight databases, it achieved 100% coverage and 100% retrieval correctness on reference schemas, with semantic role agreement between 92-100%. This addresses the knowledge-acquisition bottleneck in data analysis, where semantic layers are typically hand-written, limiting scalability and keeping non-technical users dependent on experts. By automating schema construction, TYTAN could make analytic tools more accessible and reduce errors, impacting data management and natural language interfaces. TYTAN combines symbolic analysis of the database with LLM-based inference for entity proposal, role assignment, and naming. It was evaluated on eight databases, including a blind test on a live ten-table database with no declared keys, where it recovered the full entity structure and satisfied 100% of satisfiable expectations from five independent annotators.

**Background**: Analytic semantic schemas describe the real-world entities, measures, identifiers, and relationships in a database, enabling tools like natural-language query interfaces and report generators. Traditionally, these schemas are hand-crafted, which is time-consuming and error-prone. Neurosymbolic methods combine neural networks with symbolic reasoning to produce interpretable and structured outputs, and LLMs can perform semantic inference to classify and name entities.

## [Mechanism Design for Participatory Governance of AI Agents via Compute Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06353) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a formal mechanism design model for continuous participatory governance of deployed AI agents, using resource allocation and compute budgets to enforce authorization. It proposes a two-threshold gate with hysteresis and a signed compute license to make decisions self-enforcing. This work addresses the critical challenge of governing AI agents in real-world deployments, offering a novel lever (compute budgets) that could complement existing governance frameworks. It may influence future AI safety and governance policies, especially as AI agents become more autonomous and widespread. The model frames governance as an extensive-form game where stakeholders contribute to a governance currency, and a funding aggregator converts contributions into weighted supports. A coupling map bounded by a certified safety ceiling releases a metered compute budget, realized as a signed compute license in hardware. The paper also identifies manipulation of the governing electorate by the governed agent as the central open problem.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Mechanism design is a field in economics and game theory that engineers rules to align individual incentives with desired outcomes. Participatory governance involves stakeholders in decision-making processes, and AI governance seeks to ensure AI systems operate safely and ethically. Compute budgets are a proposed lever to control AI behavior by limiting computational resources.

## [Analogy Modeled as Nonparametric Bayesian Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.04156) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes that analogy can be modeled as nonparametric Bayesian inference over relational systems, supported by behavioral experiments showing that prior relational structures bias participants' inferences in new environments. This work bridges cognitive science and machine learning, offering a formal framework for understanding human generalization that could inform the design of more human-like learning algorithms. It also provides a novel perspective on analogy, a core cognitive ability. The model generates predictions from each past environment based on their relational structures, then averages predictions according to analogical relevance. The effect of prior structures scales with the number of times the structure has been observed.

## [CoCo: Code-as-CoT Framework Boosts Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08652) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CoCo introduces a code-driven reasoning framework that uses executable code as Chain-of-Thought for text-to-image generation, enabling explicit and verifiable intermediate planning. The framework first generates code to specify the scene layout, executes it in a sandbox to render a draft image, then refines it via fine-grained editing. This work addresses the precision limitations of natural-language CoT in text-to-image generation, offering a more controllable and verifiable approach for complex layouts and rare concepts. It could influence future multimodal generation systems by demonstrating the effectiveness of code as an intermediate representation. CoCo achieves improvements of 68.83%, 54.8%, and 41.23% on StructT2IBench, OneIG-Bench, and LongText-Bench, respectively, over direct generation. The framework is supported by a curated dataset, CoCo-10K, containing structured draft-final image pairs for training.

**Background**: Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) aim to integrate understanding and generation across modalities, and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has been used to improve text-to-image generation. However, natural-language CoT lacks precision for spatial layouts and structured elements. CoCo proposes using executable code as CoT, which can be run in a sandboxed environment to produce deterministic drafts, addressing these limitations.

## [TRU: Targeted Reverse Update for Efficient Multimodal Recommendation Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02183) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces TRU, a plug-and-play unlearning framework for multimodal recommendation systems, which performs three coordinated interventions—ranking fusion gate, branch-wise modality scaling, and capacity-aware parameter-group selection—to address the non-uniform influence of deleted data across ranking behavior, modality branches, and model modules. Experiments show TRU achieves a stronger retain-forget trade-off than MMRecUn in most settings and attains favorable operating points in challenging user-level cases. This work addresses a critical privacy and compliance need in multimodal recommendation systems, where user data is tightly coupled and difficult to remove. By improving unlearning efficiency and effectiveness, TRU could enable more practical and responsible deployment of such systems, benefiting users and service providers alike. TRU is evaluated across two backbones, three datasets, and three unlearning regimes, showing superior retain-forget trade-offs. Security audits report the lowest MIA BalAcc and a tie for the lowest ASR among approximate methods in both audited settings, with wall-clock trajectories showing earlier convergence to favorable regions.

Approximate unlearning methods, such as reverse gradient updates, offer efficiency but often apply uniform updates, which may be suboptimal for complex models.

SP-Mind is introduced as the first autonomous AI agent that unifies the spatial proteomics analysis pipeline from raw multiplexed tissue imaging to phenotype discovery, without task-specific fine-tuning. It is evaluated using the newly proposed SP-Bench benchmark, which includes 102 tasks across 18 categories. This innovation addresses the fragmentation of spatial proteomics workflows, which currently require expert manual orchestration, limiting scalability and reproducibility. By automating the entire pipeline, SP-Mind could accelerate biomedical research, particularly in tumor microenvironment studies and precision medicine, and set a new standard for AI-driven analysis in this domain. SP-Mind is equipped with expert-curated biological analysis skills and specialized computational tools, converting natural-language queries into end-to-end analytical workflows. The SP-Bench benchmark spans diverse tissue types, and SP-Mind achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to existing open-source biomedical agent baselines. Code is publicly available on GitHub.

## [Gradient-Based Beam Search for D-vine Copula Fitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25020) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes a novel estimation framework that combines gradient-based maximum likelihood estimation with a beam-search strategy for fitting D-vine copulas, overcoming the limitations of greedy sequential selection. It also introduces a localized anomaly detection framework built on the fitted D-vine, providing global anomaly scores and edge-level explanations. This work addresses a known combinatorial optimization problem in vine copula fitting, enabling a broader exploration of the configuration space while remaining computationally tractable. The localized anomaly detection with uncertainty quantification could improve interpretability in multivariate statistical modeling and machine learning applications. The framework uses a fully differentiable implementation of D-vine copulas, enabling gradient-based optimization. It employs beam search to maintain multiple competing configurations, and provides statistical guarantees through Mondrian conformal prediction. The method is evaluated on benchmark and real-world datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Vine copulas are a flexible tool for modeling complex multivariate distributions by decomposing them into a hierarchy of bivariate pair-copulas. Traditional fitting methods use greedy sequential selection, which can commit to locally optimal choices and miss better global configurations. Beam search is a heuristic search algorithm that explores multiple candidate solutions simultaneously, balancing exploration and computational cost.

## [DASH: Decoupled Adaptive Surrogate-Acquisition Framework for LLM-Enhanced AutoBO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00641) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DASH is a new framework for LLM-enhanced automated Bayesian optimization that independently adapts surrogate selection and acquisition functions. It outperforms the best AutoBO baseline by 12.51% in trajectory-level Acceleration Factor and 5.00% in endpoint Enhancement Factor across four chemical optimization tasks. This work addresses a key limitation in existing AutoBO methods that either adapt only one component or jointly select pairs under a shared criterion, overlooking their distinct roles. By decoupling surrogate and acquisition adaptation, DASH improves optimization performance across tasks, which could benefit fields relying on expensive black-box optimization such as chemical engineering and materials science. DASH selects surrogates based on predictive reliability, uncertainty calibration, and ranking consistency, and uses a two-stage acquisition controller that periodically reallocates quotas across acquisition functions and delegates final selection to an LLM. It also incorporates a knowledge-guided warm start and structured memory, and ablation studies confirm the complementary contributions of all components.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sequential model-based strategy for global optimization of black-box objective functions with costly evaluations. It relies on a surrogate model to approximate the objective and an acquisition function to select the next evaluation point. Automated Bayesian optimization (AutoBO) aims to adapt these components online, but existing methods often fail to account for their distinct roles. LLM-enhanced AutoBO leverages large language models to improve component selection and adaptation.

## [Information Boundaries for Group-Robust LLM Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02940) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces information-theoretic boundaries (fiber and cone identities) to quantify uncertainty in compression scores for group-robust LLM pruning, and proposes sequential composition with a slack vector to control worst-group performance. Experiments across three dense LLMs and OLMoE show significant improvements in worst-group metrics. This work addresses a critical gap in LLM pruning: compression scores can mislead model selection when worst-group performance matters. By providing theoretical boundaries and practical methods, it enables more reliable pruning decisions for fairness-critical and safety-critical applications. The paper shows that a split-half reliable path-quadratic score predicted a 16.1% gain but selected endpoints were 6.0–7.7% worse than controls. Sequential composition adds a slack vector that determines every unrestricted one-step response, and a margin condition keeps the active group fixed along paths with bounded relative drift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: LLM pruning reduces model size by removing less important weights, but compression scores used to guide pruning may not reflect worst-group performance. Information theory provides tools to quantify uncertainty in model selection, and group robustness ensures fair performance across different subgroups. This paper combines these concepts to improve pruning decisions.

## [Surrogate Substitution Preserves PHI Detectability Across Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03172) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This study introduces a paired, multi-detector evaluation protocol using equivalence testing (TOST) to assess whether surrogate substitution in de-identified clinical text preserves PHI detectability. Across 11 detectors, 7 benchmarks, and 7 languages, recall on masked spans moved from 76.1% to 74.9%, which was statistically equivalent within a ±2-point margin (p ~ 3e-9). This work provides a rigorous, generalizable method for auditing structure-preserving de-identification transforms, which is crucial for privacy-preserving data sharing in clinical NLP. It reassures that well-formed surrogate substitution does not degrade downstream PHI detection, supporting its adoption in real-world applications. The protocol scores utility only on masked spans, decoupling coverage from utility, and uses a surrogate-failure typology to distinguish fixable generator defects from intrinsic detector limits. Residual loss concentrated in malformed and out-of-distribution surrogates (e.g., truncation 'Chicago' -> 'Illino', salience loss 'Cedars-Sinai' -> 'Vidant'), and the authors release evaluation subsets, scoring code, and an interactive dashboard.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Structure-preserving de-identification replaces protected health information (PHI) with realistic same-type surrogates (e.g., 'Anna S.' becomes 'Maria S.') to keep clinical text fluent and downstream tools functional. Equivalence testing, such as the two one-sided tests (TOST) procedure, is used to determine if two measurements are statistically equivalent within a predefined margin, unlike traditional null-hypothesis significance testing which is often uninformative at large sample sizes.

This paper introduces an information-theoretic framework to analyze the robustness-utility trade-off in black-box model watermarking, identifying a fundamental capacity crisis when relying solely on predicted labels. It proposes a new framework that leverages top-k outputs to increase effective watermarking capacity while preserving predictive performance, validated across image, text, and tabular domains. This work addresses a persistent limitation in black-box model watermarking, offering a practical solution that improves the trade-off between robustness and utility. It is significant for protecting intellectual property in machine learning, potentially enabling more reliable ownership verification without degrading model performance. The proposed method exploits the richer output space of top-k predictions to increase the effective capacity for embedding ownership signals. Experiments across multiple domains demonstrate a more favorable robustness-utility trade-off compared to existing approaches, while remaining practical for real-world deployment.

Black-box watermarking typically relies on backdoor triggers, where specific inputs cause the model to produce predefined outputs. However, these methods often degrade primary task performance due to limited capacity in predicted labels. Information theory provides a framework to quantify the maximum number of bits that can be reliably embedded, known as watermarking capacity.

The paper introduces ASAT, a human-in-the-loop framework that adaptively updates both the scoring function and threshold for out-of-distribution (OOD) detection, ensuring false positive rate (FPR) control while maximizing true positive rate (TPR). It provides theoretical guarantees for FPR control under stationary conditions and demonstrates superior performance on OpenOOD benchmarks. This work addresses a critical limitation in existing OOD detection methods—uncontrolled FPR and lack of adaptivity to evolving OOD inputs—which is essential for safety-critical ML deployments. By incorporating human feedback, ASAT offers a practical solution that can maintain reliability over time, potentially influencing future OOD detection systems in production. ASAT maximizes TPR while controlling FPR at all times under stationary conditions, and under nonstationary conditions it adapts to distribution shifts with only transient FPR violations. The framework is evaluated on OpenOOD benchmarks, showing higher TPRs while maintaining FPR control compared to existing methods.

## [GCSL-NF: Learning from Failures in Goal-Conditioned Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03206) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces GCSL with Negative Feedback (GCSL-NF), an extension to Goal-Conditioned Supervised Learning (GCSL) that evaluates each trajectory both positively (with respect to relabeled goals) and correctively (with respect to the original intended goal), using a contrastively learned similarity function instead of a reward function or geometric distance. GCSL-NF addresses two key limitations of GCSL: the exacerbation of agent biases from learning only on self-generated experiences, and the inability to learn from failures due to relabeling only successful outcomes. This could improve learning efficiency and reduce reliance on human-specified reward functions, benefiting self-adaptive autonomous systems in dynamic environments. The corrective target in GCSL-NF is derived from a similarity function learned contrastively from trajectory-induced neighborhood relations, eliminating the need for a reward function or geometric distance. Experiments show that GCSL-NF overcomes initial bias limitations, increasingly benefits from negative feedback as learning progresses, and matches or surpasses GCSL- and HER-based methods.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: Goal-Conditioned Supervised Learning (GCSL) is a self-supervised approach that trains agents to reach goals by relabeling trajectories with hindsight, learning from successful experiences. However, it suffers from biases and ignores failures. Negative feedback, as used in this work, allows agents to learn from mistakes, which is common in reinforcement learning but novel in this context. The paper builds on concepts like self-imitation learning and goal relabeling.

TimeID consists of three key components: dual-branch invariant disentangled feature learning using season-trend decomposition, lightweight parameter-free proxy denoising that calibrates LLM biases, and knowledge distillation for bidirectional alignment. The ARL paradigm simulates a dynamic curriculum of benign and malicious manipulations via an adversarial attacker agent to optimize the robustness-fragility trade-off.

**Background**: Deepfake detection is a critical security challenge as generative AI produces increasingly realistic synthetic media. Passive detectors rely on specific forgery artifacts and struggle to generalize, while proactive watermarking embeds information to identify synthetic content. Semi-fragile watermarks aim to be robust to benign transformations (e.g., compression) but fragile to malicious tampering. Latent space watermarking embeds watermarks in the latent representations of generative models, offering a unified approach across different model types. Adversarial reinforcement learning combines adversarial training with RL to make models robust against adaptive attacks.

## [Transformer Model Improves Heart Failure Discharge Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16839) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This study introduces TGDS-HF, a transformer-based method that uses short-context electronic health record (EHR) sequences to predict one-year clinical instability or mortality in heart failure patients. It was validated on a Swedish cohort of 42,820 patients, achieving AUPRCs of 0.555 and 0.574 for the two tasks. Accurate discharge planning is critical for heart failure patients, as it can reduce readmissions and improve outcomes. TGDS-HF demonstrates that transformer models can effectively leverage routinely collected EHR data, potentially enabling personalized discharge care and better resource allocation. TGDS-HF consists of three components: category-level tokenization, recency-weighted temporal representation, and sequence model configuration. Ablation studies confirmed the effectiveness of these components, and the model maintained strong performance even with reduced clinical concept availability and limited training data.

**Background**: Electronic health records (EHRs) contain longitudinal patient data that can be used for clinical prediction. Traditional machine learning models like XGBoost often require manual feature engineering, while transformer models can learn directly from sequences. This study applies a compact autoregressive transformer to short-context EHR sequences, aiming to improve discharge risk stratification for heart failure patients.

## [Conditional PAC-Efficient Reasoning in LLM Routing: Feasibility and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.03057) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper formalizes conditional PAC-efficient reasoning for LLM routing, proving that pointwise conditional control is infeasible while structured setwise control achieves finite-sample validity and near-oracle expert usage. This work provides a theoretical foundation for efficient LLM deployment, clarifying the trade-off between distribution-free reliability and computational savings. It offers a practical router design that could reduce inference costs without sacrificing performance guarantees. The proposed router achieves finite-sample conditional validity under a prespecified family of conditioning sets, and near-oracle expert usage under separation and margin conditions. The key insight is that pointwise control is too strong, whereas structured setwise control remains feasible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 7, 04:00

**Background**: PAC-efficient reasoning aims to control the performance loss when using a fast model instead of a more capable expert, with probably approximately correct (PAC) guarantees. Distribution-free risk control (DFRC) provides calibration frameworks that work without distributional assumptions. Model routing selects between models of varying capability and cost, and this paper bridges these areas by formalizing conditional efficiency.
